Hilbert expansion of the Boltzmann equation on a 2-dimensional disk with specular boundary condition
Feimin Huang, Jing Ouyang, Yong Wang

Abstract
. In the present paper, we concern the hydrodynamic limit of Boltzmann equation with specular reflection boundary condition in a two-dimensional disk to the compressible Euler equations. Due to the non-zero curvature and non-zero tangential velocity of compressible Euler solution on the boundary, new difficulties arise in the construction of Knudsen boundary layer. By employing the geometric correction, and an innovative and refined L 2 − L ∞ L^{2}-L^{\infty} method, we establish the existence and space-decay for a truncated Knudsen boundary layer. Then, by the Hilbert expansion of multi-scales, we successfully justify the hydrodynamic limit of Boltzmann equation with specular reflection boundary condition to the compressible Euler equations in the two-dimensional disk.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

The Boltzmann equation was proposed by Boltzmann
[
6
]
in 1872 to study the motion and interaction of particles, especially in non-equilibrium conditions. The seminal work by Maxwell
[
47
]
and Boltzmann
[
6
]
established the intrinsic connection between the Boltzmann equation and fluid dynamics, including both compressible and incompressible flow systems.

The hydrodynamic limit of the Boltzmann equation has been a central topic in the kinetic theory. In 1912, Hilbert introduced a systematic asymptotic expansion for the Knudsen number
\mathscr{K}_{n}\ll 1
, which significantly advanced the study of the Boltzmann equation. Later, Chapman and Enskog independently proposed a different expansion methods in 1916 and 1917. These expansion techniques have successfully derived various fluid equations, including the compressible Euler and Navier-Stokes systems, the incompressible Euler and Navier-Stokes(-Fourier) systems, as well as the acoustic system, among others.
It is still a challenging problem to rigorously justify these formal approximation, especially for the problems with physical boundaries.

Based on the truncated Hilbert expansion, Caflish
[
7
]
rigorously justified the hydrodynamic limit from the Cauchy solutions of Boltzmann equation to the compressible Euler equations under the framework of smooth solutions, see also
[
25
,
44
,
48
,
55
]
and
[
28
,
29
]
via the
L^{2}
-
L^{\infty}
method. The hydrodynamic limit of Boltzmann to wave patterns have been proved
[
34
,
36
,
37
,
38
,
61
,
62
]
in one dimensional case, see also
[
58
]
for planar rarefaction wave in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Studies on the hydrodynamic limit to acoustic equations can be found in
[
13
,
29
,
32
,
39
]
and related works.
For the derivation of incompressible Navier-Stokes system and incompressible Euler equations, we refer to
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
8
,
9
,
13
,
21
,
17
,
18
,
20
,
22
,
26
,
40
,
43
,
45
,
46
,
51
,
59
]
and references cited therein.

Most studies have been conducted in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
or
\mathbb{T}^{3}
, the investigation of the Boltzmann equation with boundary condition remains a crucial yet challenging task in the field of fluid kinetic theory. This is primarily due to the intricate dependence of boundary condition on the solution structure and the added complexity in establishing stable solutions.

In 2010, Guo
[
27
]
utilized the
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
method to demonstrate the exponential decay of solutions to the Boltzmann equation in a smooth domain with one of four common boundary conditions, see also
[
30
,
31
]
for the regularities in strictly convex domain and non-convex domain. In 2021, Guo-Huang-Wang
[
32
]
established the validity of the Hilbert expansion for the Boltzmann equation with specular reflection boundary condition, which leads to derivations of compressible Euler equations and acoustic equations in half-space.
Later, Jiang-Luo-Tang
[
41
]
derived the hydrodynamic limit from the Boltzmann equation with diffuse reflection boundary condition to the compressible Euler equations in half-space.

In 2016, by introducing a geometrically modified Knudsen boundary layer, Wu
[
59
]
justified the hydrodynamic limit of the Boltzmann equation with an incoming boundary condition to the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations in a two-dimensional disk, see very recent results
[
49
,
50
]
on general smooth domains. We remark that the geometric correction was firstly introduced in the study of neutral transport equation
[
60
]
.

We point out that there is no geometric influence in
[
32
,
41
]
for the compressible Euler limit of Boltzmann equation since the boundary of half-space is flat. In this paper, we mainly concern the hydrodynamic limit of Boltzmann equation with specular reflection boundary condition to the compressible Euler equations within a two-dimensional disk, and the interaction between non-zero boundary curvature and non-zero tangential flow velocity of compressible Euler equations introduces new challenges in analyzing Knudsen boundary layer problems.

In this paper, we consider the Boltzmann equation

(1.1)

F_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F=\frac{1}{\mathscr{K}_{n}}Q(F,F),

where
F(t,x,v)
is the density distribution function for the gas particles with position
x\in B_{1}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|x|\leq 1\}
and velocity
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
at time
t\geq 0
. The Boltzmann collision term
Q(F,G)
is defined by

(1.2)

\displaystyle Q(F,G)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}B(|v-u|,\omega)[F(v^{\prime})G(u^{\prime})-F(v)G(u)]dud\omega,

where the relationship between the post-collision velocity
v^{\prime},u^{\prime}
of two particles with the pre-collision velocity
v,u
is given by

\displaystyle u^{\prime}=u+[(v-u)\cdot\omega]\cdot\omega,\quad v^{\prime}=v-[(v-u)\cdot\omega]\cdot\omega,

for
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, which can be determined by conservation laws of momentum and energy

\displaystyle v^{\prime}+u^{\prime}=v+u,\quad|v^{\prime}|^{2}+|u^{\prime}|^{2}=|v|^{2}+|u|^{2}.

In the present paper, we consider hard sphere case, i.e.

(1.3)

\displaystyle B(|v-u|,\omega)=|(v-u)\cdot\omega|.

For later use, we divide the boundary
\gamma:=\partial B_{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
into incoming boundary
\gamma_{-}
, outgoing boundary
\gamma_{+}
and grazing set
\gamma_{0}
:

\displaystyle\gamma_{-}=\{(x,v)\in\partial B_{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}:v\cdot\vec{n}\textless 0\},

(1.4)

\displaystyle\gamma_{+}=\{(x,v)\in\partial B_{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}:v\cdot\vec{n}\textgreater 0\},

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}=\{(x,v)\in\partial B_{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}:v\cdot\vec{n}=0\},

where
\vec{n}
is the outgoing normal vector of
B_{1}
. In the present paper, we adopt the specular reflection boundary condition, i.e.

(1.5)

\displaystyle F(t,x,v)|_{\gamma_{-}}=F(t,x,R_{x}v),\quad\text{ where }R_{x}v=v-2(v\cdot\vec{n})\vec{n}.

1.2.
Formal Hilbert expansion

Inspired by
[
32
]
, we know that the thickness of viscous boundary layer is
\sqrt{\mathscr{K}_{n}}
.
For later use, we denote
\varepsilon:=\sqrt{\mathscr{K}_{n}}
and
F
as
F^{\varepsilon}
. Then (
1.1
) is rewritten as

(1.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F^{\varepsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F^{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}Q(F^{\varepsilon},F^{\varepsilon}).

1.2.1.
Formal internal expansion

Define the internal expansion by
F^{\varepsilon}\sim\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{k}F_{k}(t,x,v)
, then one has

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{k}(\partial_{t}F_{k}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}Q(\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{k}F_{k},\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{k}F_{k})

(1.7)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{l=0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{i,j\geq 0,\atop i+j=l}\varepsilon^{l-2}Q(F_{i},F_{j}).

Comparing the coefficients to each order of
\varepsilon
, one obtains the following equations:

(1.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\varepsilon^{-2}:\quad 0=Q(F_{0},F_{0}),\\
&\varepsilon^{-1}:\quad 0=Q(F_{0},F_{1})+Q(F_{1},F_{0}),\\
&\varepsilon^{0}:\quad\{\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\}F_{0}=Q(F_{0},F_{2})+Q(F_{2},F_{0})+Q(F_{1},F_{1}),\\
&\vdots\\
&\varepsilon^{k}:\quad\{\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\}F_{k}=Q(F_{0},F_{k+2})+Q(F_{k+2},F_{0})+\sum\limits_{i,j\geq 1\atop i+j=k+2}Q(F_{i},F_{j}),\quad\text{for}\,k\geq 0.\end{split}

It follows from
\eqref{ie}_{1}
and the celebrated H-theorem that
F_{0}
is a local Maxwellian:

\mu(t,x,v):=F_{0}(t,x,v)\equiv\frac{\rho(t,x)}{2\pi T(t,x)}\exp\left\{-\frac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{2T(t,x)}\right\},

where

\displaystyle\rho=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F_{0}dv,\quad\rho\mathfrak{u}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}v\cdot F_{0}dv,\quad\rho|\mathfrak{u}|^{2}+2\rho T=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|v|^{2}F_{0}dv,

which represent macroscopic density, velocity and temperature.

Multiplying
\eqref{ie}_{3}
by
1,v,|v|^{2}
, which are four collision invariants for the Boltzmann collision operator
Q(\cdot,\cdot)
, one obtains that
(\rho,\mathfrak{u},T)
satisfies the compressible Euler system

(1.9)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\text{div}(\rho\mathfrak{u})=0,\\
\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho\mathfrak{u})+\text{div}(\rho\mathfrak{u}\otimes\mathfrak{u})+\nabla(\rho T)=0,\\
\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{\rho|\mathfrak{u}|^{2}+2\rho T}{2}\right)+\text{div}\left(\mathfrak{u}\cdot\frac{(\rho|\mathfrak{u}|^{2}+2\rho T)}{2}\right)+\text{div}(\mathfrak{u}\cdot\rho T)=0.\end{cases}

Usually, we denote
p:=\rho T
to be the pressure function. For (
1.9
), we consider the slip boundary

(1.10)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}\cdot\vec{n}=0,

which satisfies the specular reflection boundary condition, i.e.,
F_{0}(t,x,v)|_{\gamma_{-}}=F_{0}(t,x,R_{x}v)
. The initial data is given by

(1.11)

\displaystyle(\rho,\mathfrak{u},T)(0,x)=(1+\delta\phi_{0},\delta\Phi_{0},1+\delta\vartheta_{0})(x),

with
\|(\phi_{0},\Phi_{0},\vartheta_{0})\|_{H^{s_{0}}}\leq 1
, where
0<\delta\ll 1
is a parameter and
s_{0}\geq 2
is some given number. Then
1+\delta\phi_{0},1+\delta\vartheta_{0}
are positive. There is a family of local-in-time classical solutions
(\rho^{\delta},\mathfrak{u}^{\delta},T^{\delta})\in C([0,\tau^{\delta}];H^{s_{0}}(B_{1}))\cap C^{1}([0,\tau^{\delta}];H^{s_{0}-1-\delta}(B_{1}))
of the compressible Euler equations (
1.9
)–(
1.11
) such that
\rho^{\delta}>0,T^{\delta}>0
, here the life-span
\tau^{\delta}>0
depends on
\delta^{-1}
; see Lemma
2.1
.

For later use, we define the linear collision operator
\mathbf{L}
by

(1.12)

\mathbf{L}g=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}\{Q(\mu,\sqrt{\mu}g)+Q(\sqrt{\mu}g,\mu)\},

and denote the null space of
\mathbf{L}
as
\mathcal{N}
which is generated by

(1.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\chi_{0}(v)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho}}\sqrt{\mu},\\
&\chi_{i}(v)=\frac{v_{i}-\mathfrak{u}_{i}}{\sqrt{\rho T}}\sqrt{\mu},\quad i=1,2,\\
&\chi_{3}(v)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\rho}}\left\{\frac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{T}-2\right\}\sqrt{\mu}.\end{split}

For
k\geq 1
, we define the macroscopic part and the microscopic part of
f_{k}:=\frac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu}}
as
\mathbf{P}f_{k}
and
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P})f_{k}
where
\mathbf{P}
is the projection operator on
\mathcal{N}
. We denote

\displaystyle\rho_{k}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}F_{k}\,dv,\quad\rho u_{k}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(v-\mathfrak{u})\cdot F_{k}\,dv,\quad\rho\theta_{k}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}-2T\right)F_{k}\,dv.

then we obtain from (
1.8
) that

\displaystyle\frac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\left\{\frac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu}}\right\}+\{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}\}\Big\{\frac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu}}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{\rho_{k}}{\sqrt{\rho}}\chi_{0}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{T}}u_{k,j}\cdot\chi_{j}+\frac{\sqrt{\rho}}{2}\frac{\theta_{k}}{T}\chi_{3}\right\}+\{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}\}\Big\{\frac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu}}\Big\}

(1.14)

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{\rho_{k}}{\rho}+\frac{u_{k}}{T}\cdot(v-\mathfrak{u})+\frac{\theta_{k}}{4T}\cdot\left(\frac{|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}}{T}-2\right)\right\}\sqrt{\mu}+\{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}\}\Big\{\frac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu}}\Big\},

with

(1.15)

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}\}\Big\{\frac{F_{k+1}}{\sqrt{\mu}}\Big\}=\mathbf{L}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}\left[-\{\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\}F_{k-1}+\sum\limits_{i,j\geq 1,\atop i+j=k+1}Q(F_{i},F_{j})\right]\right\}.

It is clear from (
1.15
) that
\{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}\}\left\{\frac{F_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu}}\right\}
is determined by
F_{i},0\leq i\leq k-1
. Also it follows from
\eqref{ie}_{2}
that
f_{1}\in\mathcal{N}
.

Motivated by
[
32
]
, we shall construct
F_{k}
by solving a hyperbolic boundary value problem, see Sections 2.1 and
3.1
.

1.2.2.
Formal viscous boundary layer expansion

We remark that, in general, the internal solutions
F_{0},F_{1},...,F_{k}
in Section 1.2.1 do not satisfy the specular boundary condition (
1.5
). Motivated by
[
32
,
54
]
, we introduce the viscous boundary layers near
\partial B_{1}
. Since the viscous boundary layers change dramatically along the normal direction, it is necessary to use the polar coordinate, i.e.

\displaystyle x_{1}=r\cos\phi,\quad x_{2}=r\sin\phi,\quad(r,\phi)\in[0,1]\times[0,2\pi).

Then we make change of variables
(r,\phi,v)\rightarrow(y,\phi,\bar{v})
that

\displaystyle y=\frac{1-r}{\varepsilon},\quad\bar{v}=-v.

We remark that the scaled normal coordinate
y
is essential for the viscous boundary layer. Then it is clear that

\displaystyle v\cdot\nabla_{x}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{n})\partial_{y}-\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon y}(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{\tau})\partial_{\phi},

where
\vec{n}=(\cos\phi,\sin\phi),\vec{\tau}=(-\sin\phi,\cos\phi)
.

We consider the viscous boundary layer in the following form

(1.16)

\displaystyle\bar{F}^{\varepsilon}(t,y,\phi,\bar{v})\sim\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{k}\bar{F}_{k}(t,y,\phi,\bar{v}).

Then substituting
F^{\varepsilon}+\bar{F}^{\varepsilon}
into (
1.6
), and using (
1.7
), one obtains

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{F}^{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{n})\partial_{y}\bar{F}^{\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon y}(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{\tau})\partial_{\phi}\bar{F}^{\varepsilon}

(1.17)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\big[Q(F^{\varepsilon},\bar{F}^{\varepsilon})+Q(\bar{F}^{\varepsilon},F^{\varepsilon})+Q(\bar{F}^{\varepsilon},\bar{F}^{\varepsilon})\big].

For later use, we need to write
F_{k}(t,x,v)
to be
F_{k}(t,r,\bar{v})
in the polar coordinate. Since the scale of normal variables of
F_{k}
and
\bar{F}_{k}
are different, we take Taylor expansion for
F_{k}
at
r=1
:

(1.18)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu(t,r,\phi,v)&=\mu_{0}+\sum\limits_{l=1}^{\mathfrak{b}}\frac{(-\varepsilon y)^{l}}{l!}\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0}+\frac{(-\varepsilon y)^{\mathfrak{b}+1}}{(\mathfrak{b}+1)!}\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\tilde{\mu},\\
F_{k}(t,r,\phi,v)&=F_{k}^{0}+\sum\limits_{l=1}^{\mathfrak{b}}\frac{(-\varepsilon y)^{l}}{l!}\partial_{r}^{l}F_{k}^{0}+\frac{(-\varepsilon y)^{\mathfrak{b}+1}}{(\mathfrak{b}+1)!}\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\mathcal{F}_{k},\quad k\geq 1,\end{split}

where we have denoted
\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0}:=\partial_{r}^{l}\mu|_{r=1}
and
\partial_{r}^{l}F_{k}^{0}:=\partial_{r}^{l}F_{k}|_{r=1},l=0,1,...
It is clear that

(1.19)

\displaystyle\mu_{0}(t,\phi,\bar{v})=\frac{\rho^{0}}{2\pi T^{0}}e^{-\frac{|\bar{v}+\mathfrak{u}^{0}|^{2}}{2T^{0}}}=\frac{\rho^{0}}{2\pi T^{0}}e^{-\frac{|\bar{v}-\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}|^{2}}{2T^{0}}},

where
(\rho^{0},\mathfrak{u}^{0},T^{0})=(\rho,\mathfrak{u},T)(t,r,\phi)|_{r=1}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}=-\mathfrak{u}^{0}
. The notations
\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\tilde{\mu}
and
\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\mathcal{F}_{k}
are the corresponding Taylor remainders.

Comparing the order of
\varepsilon
in (
1.17
), one gets that

(1.20)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varepsilon^{-1}:&\quad 0=Q(\mu_{0},\bar{F}_{1})+Q(\bar{F}_{1},\mu_{0}),\\
\varepsilon^{0}:&\quad(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{n})\partial_{y}\bar{F}_{1}=Q(\mu_{0},\bar{F}_{2})+Q(\bar{F}_{2},\mu_{0})+Q(F_{1}^{0},\bar{F}_{1})+Q(\bar{F}_{1},F_{1}^{0})\\
&\quad+Q(-y\partial_{r}\mu_{0},\bar{F}_{1})+Q(\bar{F}_{1},-y\partial_{r}\mu_{0})+Q(\bar{F}_{1},\bar{F}_{1}),\\
&\quad\vdots\\
\varepsilon^{k}:&\quad(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{n})\partial_{y}\bar{F}_{k+1}=Q(\mu_{0},\bar{F}_{k+2})+Q(\bar{F}_{k+2},\mu_{0})\\
&\quad+\sum\limits_{l,j\geq 1\atop l+j=k+2}(-1)^{l}\frac{y^{l}}{l!}[Q(\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0},\bar{F}_{j})+Q(\bar{F}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0})]+\sum\limits_{l,j\geq 1\atop l+j=k+2}[Q(F_{l}^{0},\bar{F}_{j})+Q(\bar{F}_{j},F_{l}^{0})]\\
&\quad+\sum\limits_{i,l,j\geq 1\atop i+l+j=k+2}(-1)^{l}\frac{y^{l}}{l!}[Q(\partial_{r}^{l}F_{i}^{0},\bar{F}_{j})+Q(\bar{F}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}F_{i}^{0})]+\sum\limits_{i,j\geq 1\atop i+j=k+2}Q(\bar{F}_{j},\bar{F}_{i})\\
&\quad-\partial_{t}\bar{F}_{k}+\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon y}(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{\tau})\partial_{\phi}\bar{F}_{k}.\end{split}

Since
\bar{F}_{k}
is mainly used to compensate the boundary condition, it is natural to assume

(1.21)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{y\rightarrow\infty}\bar{F}_{k}=0.

Noting that
\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon y}
has singularity at
y=\varepsilon^{-1}
, we define
\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}
to be the solution of the following approximate problem

\displaystyle(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{n})\partial_{y}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k+1}=Q(\mu_{0},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k+2})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k+2},\mu_{0})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum\limits_{l,j\geq 1\atop l+j=k+2}(-1)^{l}\frac{y^{l}}{l!}[Q(\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0})]+\sum\limits_{l,j\geq 1\atop l+j=k+2}[Q(F_{l}^{0},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},F_{l}^{0})]

\displaystyle\quad+\sum\limits_{i,l,j\geq 1\atop i+l+j=k+2}(-1)^{l}\frac{y^{l}}{l!}[Q(\partial_{r}^{l}F_{i}^{0},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}F_{i}^{0})]+\sum\limits_{i,j\geq 1\atop i+j=k+2}Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i})

(1.22)

\displaystyle\quad-\partial_{t}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}+\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{\tau})\partial_{\phi}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k},

where
\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)=\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon y}\Upsilon(\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}y)
with
\Upsilon(z)
being a smooth and monotone function such that

\displaystyle\Upsilon(z)=\begin{cases}1,\quad z\leq\frac{1}{2},\\
0,\quad z\geq 1.\end{cases}

For later use, we define

(1.23)

\begin{split}\mathbf{L}_{0}(t,\phi,\bar{v}):=\mathbf{L}(t,\phi,y,\bar{v})|_{y=0},\\
\quad\mathbf{P}_{0}(t,\phi,\bar{v}):=\mathbf{P}(t,\phi,y,\bar{v})|_{y=0},\\
\mathcal{N}_{0}(t,\phi,y,\bar{v}):=\mathcal{N}(t,\phi,y,\bar{v})|_{y=0}.\end{split}

For
k\geq 1
, we define
\bar{f}_{k}:=\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}
, and consider the decomposition

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}=\mathbf{P}_{0}\left\{\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}\right\}+\{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{0}\}\left\{\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}\right\}

(1.24)

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{\bar{\rho}_{k}}{\rho^{0}}+\frac{\bar{u}_{k}}{T^{0}}\cdot(\bar{v}-\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0})+\frac{\bar{\theta}_{k}}{4T^{0}}\cdot\left(\frac{|\bar{v}-\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}|^{2}}{T^{0}}-2\right)\right\}\sqrt{\mu_{0}}+\{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{0}\}\left\{\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}\right\},

where

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{k}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}\,dv,\quad\rho^{0}\bar{u}_{k}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\bar{v}-\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0})\cdot\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}\,dv,\quad\rho^{0}\bar{\theta}_{k}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(|\bar{v}-\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}|^{2}-2T^{0}\right)\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}\,dv,

and

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{0}\}\left\{\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k+1}}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}\right\}=\mathbf{L}_{0}^{-1}\left\{\frac{-(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{n})\partial_{y}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}+\sum_{i,j\geq 1,\atop i+j=k+1}Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j})}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+\sum_{l,j\geq 1\atop l+j=k+1}(-1)^{l}\frac{y^{l}}{l!}\frac{[Q(\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0})]}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}+\sum_{l,j\geq 1\atop l+j=k+1}\frac{[Q(F_{l}^{0},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},F_{l}^{0})]}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}\right.

(1.25)

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+\sum_{i,l,j\geq 1\atop i+l+j=k+1}(-1)^{l}\frac{y^{l}}{l!}\frac{[Q(\partial_{r}^{l}F_{i}^{0},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}F_{i}^{0})]}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}-\frac{\partial_{t}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k-1}-\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{\tau})\partial_{\phi}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k-1}}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}\right\}.

It is clear from (
1.25
) that
\{\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{0}\}\left\{\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k+1}}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}\right\}
is determined by
F_{i},0\leq i\leq k
and
\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},1\leq j\leq k
. Noting
\eqref{vs}_{1}
, one has
\bar{f}_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
.

Motivated by
[
32
]
, we shall construct
\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}
by solving a parabolic system, see Sections 2.2 and
3.2
for more details. Once we obtain
\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}
, then we further define approximate the viscous layer

(1.26)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{F}}^{\varepsilon}(t,y,\phi,\bar{v})\sim\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}(t,y,\phi,\bar{v}).

We remark that, although the approximate viscous layer
\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}
does not exactly solve the original problem (
1.20
), but it is enough because the error has a very high order
\varepsilon
-decay rate due to the space decay.

1.2.3.
Formal Knudsen boundary layer expansion

From (
1.15
) and (
1.25
), we notice that
F^{\varepsilon}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}^{\varepsilon}
may still not satisfy the specular boundary. In fact, both the internal solutions and the viscous boundary layers (governed by parabolic and hyperbolic systems respectively) only carry finite-dimensional information of
v
. To capture the remaining infinite dimensional information of
v
, it is essential to introduce the Knudsen layer, which is in fact a steady Boltzmann equation, see
[
32
,
54
]
.

For the Knudsen boundary layer, we define the new scaled normal coordinate as

\eta:=\frac{y}{\varepsilon}=\frac{1-r}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

Following
[
49
,
59
]
, due to the geometric effect of the disk and the poor normal regularity of Boltzmann equation, it is necessary to select the essential tangential variable to establish the high order Hilbert expansion. Thus we define

\mathfrak{v}:=(\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2})=(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{n},\bar{v}\cdot\vec{\tau}),

which yields
\partial_{\phi}\rightarrow\partial_{\phi}+\mathfrak{v}_{2}\partial_{\mathfrak{v}_{1}}-\mathfrak{v}_{1}\partial_{\mathfrak{v}_{2}}
and

\displaystyle v\cdot\nabla_{x}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathfrak{v}_{1}\partial_{\eta}-\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\left(\mathfrak{v}_{2}\partial_{\phi}+\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\mathfrak{v}_{1}}}-\mathfrak{v}_{2}\mathfrak{v}_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\mathfrak{v}_{2}}}\right).

Inspired by
[
32
,
54
]
, we consider the Knudsen layer of the following form

(1.27)

\displaystyle\hat{F}^{\varepsilon}(t,\eta,\phi,\mathfrak{v})\sim\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{k}\hat{F}_{k}(t,\eta,\phi,\mathfrak{v}).

Substituting
F^{\varepsilon}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}^{\varepsilon}+\hat{F}^{\varepsilon}
to (
1.6
), using (
1.7
) and (
1.22
), and ignoring some terms with high order of
\varepsilon
, one has

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{F}^{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathfrak{v}_{1}\partial_{\eta}\hat{F}^{\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\left(\mathfrak{v}_{2}\partial_{\phi}\hat{F}^{\varepsilon}+\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial\hat{F}^{\varepsilon}}{\partial{\mathfrak{v}_{1}}}-\mathfrak{v}_{2}\mathfrak{v}_{1}\frac{\partial\hat{F}^{\varepsilon}}{\partial{\mathfrak{v}_{2}}}\right)

(1.28)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}[Q(\hat{F}^{\varepsilon},F^{\varepsilon}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}^{\varepsilon})+Q(F^{\varepsilon}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}^{\varepsilon},\hat{F}^{\varepsilon})+Q(\hat{F}^{\varepsilon},\hat{F}^{\varepsilon})].

Similarly, we need the Taylor expansions of
\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}
near
y=0
:

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}=\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}^{0}+\sum\limits_{l=1}^{\mathfrak{b}}\frac{(\varepsilon\eta)^{l}}{l!}\partial_{y}^{l}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}^{0}+\frac{(\varepsilon\eta)^{\mathfrak{b}+1}}{(\mathfrak{b}+1)!}\partial_{y}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{k},

where
\partial_{y}^{l}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}^{0}:=\partial_{y}^{l}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}|_{y=0},l=0,1,\cdots
and
\partial_{y}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
is the remainder. Also, the internal solutions should also be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mu=\mu_{0}+\sum\limits_{l=1}^{\mathfrak{b}}\frac{(-\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{l}}{l!}\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0}+\frac{(-\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{\mathfrak{b}+1}}{(\mathfrak{b}+1)!}\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\tilde{\mu},

\displaystyle F_{k}=F_{k}^{0}+\sum\limits_{l=1}^{\mathfrak{b}}\frac{(-\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{l}}{l!}\partial_{r}^{l}F_{k}^{0}+\frac{(-\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{\mathfrak{b}+1}}{(\mathfrak{b}+1)!}\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\mathcal{F}_{k},

where we point out that
\mu_{0}
in (
1.19
) can also be written as
\mu_{0}=\frac{\rho^{0}}{2\pi T^{0}}\exp\big\{-\frac{|\mathfrak{v}-\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}|^{2}}{2T^{0}}\big\}
with
\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}=(0,u_{\tau}^{0})
and
u_{\tau}^{0}=\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\cdot\vec{\tau}
.

As pointed out in
[
59
]
, it is hard to make high order expansion if the geometric correction is ignored. Hence, we compare the order of
\varepsilon
in (
1.28
) and take the geometric effect into account, one obtains that

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-1}:

\displaystyle\quad\mathfrak{v}_{1}\partial_{\eta}\hat{F}_{1}-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\left\{\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial\hat{F}_{1}}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{1}}-\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\frac{\partial\hat{F}_{1}}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{2}}\right\}=Q(\mu_{0},\hat{F}_{1})+Q(\hat{F}_{1},\mu_{0}),

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{0}:

\displaystyle\quad\mathfrak{v}_{1}\partial_{\eta}\hat{F}_{2}-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\left\{\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial\hat{F}_{2}}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{1}}-\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\frac{\partial\hat{F}_{2}}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{2}}\right\}=Q(\mu_{0},\hat{F}_{2})+Q(\hat{F}_{2},\mu_{0})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+Q(F_{1}^{0},\hat{F}_{1})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}^{0},\hat{F}_{1})+Q(\hat{F}_{1},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}^{0})+Q(\hat{F}_{1},\hat{F}_{1}),

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{1}:

\displaystyle\quad\mathfrak{v}_{1}\partial_{\eta}\hat{F}_{3}-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\left\{\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial\hat{F}_{3}}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{1}}-\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\frac{\partial\hat{F}_{3}}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{2}}\right\}=Q(\mu_{0},\hat{F}_{3})+Q(\hat{F}_{3},\mu_{0})

\displaystyle\qquad+Q(F_{1}^{0},\hat{F}_{2})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}^{0},\hat{F}_{2})+Q(\hat{F}_{2},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}^{0})+Q(\hat{F}_{2},\hat{F}_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad+Q(F_{2}^{0}-\eta\partial_{r}\mu_{0}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{2}^{0}+\eta\partial_{y}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}^{0},\hat{F}_{1})+Q(\hat{F}_{1},F_{2}^{0}-\eta\partial_{r}\mu_{0}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{2}^{0}+\eta\partial_{y}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{1}^{0})

(1.29)

\displaystyle\qquad+Q(\hat{F}_{1},\hat{F}_{2})+Q(\hat{F}_{2},\hat{F}_{1})-\partial_{t}\hat{F}_{1}+\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\partial_{\phi}\hat{F}_{1},

\displaystyle...

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{k}:

\displaystyle\quad\mathfrak{v}_{1}\partial_{\eta}\hat{F}_{k+2}-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\left\{\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial\hat{F}_{k+2}}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{1}}-\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\frac{\partial\hat{F}_{k+2}}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{2}}\right\}=Q(\mu_{0},\hat{F}_{k+2})+Q(\hat{F}_{k+2},\mu_{0})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{l,j\geq 1\atop 2l+j=k+2}\frac{(-\eta)^{l}}{l!}[Q(\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0})]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{i,l,j\geq 1\atop 2l+i+j=k+2}\frac{(-\eta)^{l}}{l!}[Q(\partial_{r}^{l}F_{i}^{0},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}F_{i}^{0})]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{i,j\geq 1\atop i+j=k+2}[Q(F_{i}^{0}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}^{0},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},F_{i}^{0}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}^{0})]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{i,l,j\geq 1\atop l+i+j=k+2}\frac{\eta^{l}}{l!}[Q(\partial_{y}^{l}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}^{0},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},\partial_{y}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}^{0})]+\sum\limits_{i,j\geq 1\atop i+j=k+2}Q(\hat{F}_{i},\hat{F}_{j})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\partial_{t}\hat{F}_{k}+\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\partial_{\phi}\hat{F}_{k}.

For later use, we can write the equation of
\hat{F}_{k}
in (
1.29
) in the following general form

(1.30)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}_{1}\partial_{\eta}\hat{F}_{k}-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\left\{\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial\hat{F}_{k}}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{1}}-\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\frac{\partial\hat{F}_{k}}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{2}}\right\}-[Q(\mu_{0},\hat{F}_{k})+Q(\hat{F}_{k},\mu_{0})]=\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{k},

where

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=k\\
i,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}[Q(F^{0}_{i}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}^{0},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},F^{0}_{i}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}^{0})+Q(\hat{F}_{i},\hat{F}_{j})]

\displaystyle\quad+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j+2l=k\\
b\geq l\geq 1,j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{(-\eta)^{l}}{l!}\cdot[Q(\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0})]

\displaystyle\quad+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+2l=k\\
i,j\geq 1,b\geq l\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{(-\eta)^{l}}{l!}\cdot Q(\partial_{r}^{l}F^{0}_{i},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}F^{0}_{i})]

\displaystyle\quad+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+l=k\\
i,j\geq 1,b\geq l\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{\eta^{l}}{l!}[Q(\partial_{y}^{l}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}^{0},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},\partial_{y}^{l}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}^{0})]

(1.31)

\displaystyle\quad-\partial_{t}\hat{F}_{k-2}+\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\partial_{\phi}\hat{F}_{k-2}.

From the last term on RHS of (
1.31
), the estimate on
\partial_{\phi}\hat{F}_{k-2}
is needed.
It is noted that
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{k}
is determined by
F_{i},0\leq i\leq k-1
and
\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},\hat{F}_{j},1\leq j\leq k-1
, and thus it can be regarded as known functions when solving
\hat{F}_{k}
.
To solve (
1.30
), we denote
\hat{f}_{k}:=\frac{\hat{F}_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}
, then (
1.30
) is rewritten as

(1.32)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}_{1}\partial_{\eta}\hat{f}_{k}-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\left\{\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial\hat{f}_{k}}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{1}}-\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\frac{\partial\hat{f}_{k}}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{2}}\right\}+\frac{u_{\tau}^{0}}{2T^{0}}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\hat{f}_{k}+\mathbf{L}_{0}\hat{f}_{k}=\frac{\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{k}}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}=:\hat{S}_{k}.

The Knudsen boundary layer problem (
1.32
) (or equivalent (
1.30
)) is indeed a steady Boltzmann equation with geometric correction. Extensive research has been conducted on classical Knudsen boundary layers(the curvature of domain is zero, then no geometric correction term arises). The seminal work by Bardos et al.
[
1
]
established existence, uniqueness, and asymptotic behavior properties for linearized Boltzmann equations under Dirichlet-type boundary condition, see also
[
11
]
for a classification of Knudsen layer problems. Later, the existence and stability were proved in
[
56
,
57
]
where the Mach number of at far field plays an important role. For the diffuse boundary condition, the existence of steady Boltzmann solution is proved in
[
14
,
19
,
35
]
. For the specular reflection condition, Golse-Perthame-Sulem
[
24
]
proved the existence, uniqueness and asymptotic behavior in a certain functional space. Huang-Jiang-Wang
[
33
]
obtained the existence and exponential space decay of steady boundary layer solution in
L^{2}_{x,v}\cap L^{\infty}_{x,v}.
We also refer to
[
5
]
for the boundary layer with transition,
[
12
]
for pointwise estimate.

For non-flat domain, the Knudsen boundary layer with geometric correction plays an important role in the hydrodynamic limit of Boltzmann equation. In fact, for the case of
u^{0}_{\tau}=0
, Wu
[
59
]
established the existence and space decay of Knudsen boundary layer solution of (
1.32
) with inflow boundary condition, then successfully justified the hydrodynamic limit of Boltzmann equation to the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations in 2D disk.

In the case of compressible Euler framework, it is not reasonable to assume
u_{\tau}^{0}\equiv 0
in general. We point out that it is very hard to solve the Knudsen boundary layer problem (
1.32
) in the case of
u_{\tau}^{0}\neq 0
because a serious difficulty arises from
\frac{u_{\tau}^{0}}{2T^{0}}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\hat{f}_{k}
in the
L^{2}
energy estimate.
Such term also appears in the study of shear flow
[
15
,
16
]
, but only polynomial decay of particle velocity are obtained, and it is hard for us to construct the Knudsen boundary layers of high order.
In fact, one of the core contributions of this paper is to establish the well-posedness and space decay of above Knudsen layer problem with
u_{\tau}^{0}\neq 0
.

Due to the new term
\frac{u_{\tau}^{0}}{2T^{0}}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\hat{f}_{k}
, it is hard to solve it in
\eta\in(0,+\infty)
.
By noting that the Knudsen layer only plays a key role near the boundary, then it should be enough to consider a truncated problem on
\eta\in(0,d)
.
That is

(1.33)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}_{1}\partial_{\eta}f+G(\eta)\left(\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{1}}-\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{2}}\right)-\frac{u_{\tau}^{0}}{2T^{0}}\cdot G(\eta)\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}f+\mathbf{L}_{0}f=\mathfrak{S},\\
\displaystyle f(0,\mathfrak{v})|_{\mathfrak{v}_{1}>0}=f(0,R\mathfrak{v})+f_{b}(R\mathfrak{v}),\\
\displaystyle f(d,\mathfrak{v})|_{\mathfrak{v}_{1}<0}=0,\end{cases}

where
G(\eta):=-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}
,
\eta\in(0,d)
with
d=\varepsilon^{-\mathfrak{a}}
,
R\mathfrak{v}=(-\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2})
. The function
f_{b}(\mathfrak{v}
) is only defined for
\mathfrak{v}_{1}\textless 0
, and we always assume that it is extended to be
0
for
\mathfrak{v}_{1}>0
. Since we hope that
f(\eta,\mathfrak{v})
has fast decay with respect to
\eta
, it is reasonable to give the boundary condition
f(d,\mathfrak{v})|_{\mathfrak{v}_{1}<0}=0
, in fact it plays an important role in the space decay estimate.

We shall apply the
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
-estimate developed in
[
27
]
to study the well-posedness of Knudsen layer problem (
1.33
). For later use, we introduce a global Maxwellian

\displaystyle\mu_{M}:=\frac{1}{2\pi T_{M}}\exp\{-\frac{|v|^{2}}{2T_{M}}\},

where

(1.34)

\displaystyle 0<T_{M}<\min_{x\in B_{1}}T(t,x)\leq\max_{x\in B_{1}}T(t,x)\textless 2T_{M}.

Under assumption (
1.34
), it is easy to know that there exist positive constant
C>0
and
\frac{1}{2}<\iota<1
such that

(1.35)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C}\mu_{M}\leq\mu(t,x,v)\leq C\mu_{M}^{\iota}.

We define weight function

\displaystyle w_{\beta}=(1+|\mathfrak{v}|^{2})^{\frac{\beta}{2}}e^{\zeta|\mathfrak{v}|^{2}},

where
0<\zeta<\frac{1}{4T_{M}}
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\beta>3
. Assume
\mathfrak{S}\in\mathcal{N}_{0}^{\perp}
and there exists a positive constant
\sigma_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle\Big\|\nu^{-1}w_{\beta}e^{\sigma_{0}\eta}\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}\mathfrak{S}\Big\|_{L_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}^{\infty}}+\Big|w_{\beta+1}\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}f_{b}\Big|_{L_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\infty}}

(1.36)

\displaystyle+\Big|\nu^{-1}w_{\beta}\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}(\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{1}}-\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{2}})f_{b}\Big|_{L^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{v}}}<\infty.

Suppose

(1.37)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathfrak{v}_{1}f_{b}(\mathfrak{v})\sqrt{\mu_{0}}\,d\mathfrak{v}\equiv 0,\\
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}f_{b}(\mathfrak{v})\sqrt{\mu_{0}}\,d\mathfrak{v}\equiv 0,\\
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathfrak{v}_{1}|\mathfrak{v}|^{2}f_{b}(\mathfrak{v})\sqrt{\mu_{0}}\,d\mathfrak{v}\equiv 0.\end{split}

Then the problem (
1.33
) has a unique solution
f
satisfying

\displaystyle\Big\|w_{\beta}e^{\sigma\eta}\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}f(\cdot,\cdot)\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}+\Big|w_{\beta}e^{\sigma\eta}\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}f(0,\cdot)\Big|_{L^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{v}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sigma(\sigma_{0}-\sigma)}\bigg\{\Big\|\nu^{-1}w_{\beta}e^{\sigma_{0}\eta}\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}\mathfrak{S}\Big\|_{L_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}^{\infty}}+\Big|w_{\beta+1}\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}f_{b}\Big|_{L_{\mathfrak{v}}^{\infty}}

(1.38)

\displaystyle\quad+\Big|\nu^{-1}w_{\beta}\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}(\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{1}}-\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{2}})f_{b}\Big|_{L^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{v}}}\bigg\},

where
C>0
is a constant independent of
\varepsilon
and
\sigma\in(0,\sigma_{0})
. Moreover, if
\mathfrak{S}
is continuous in
[0,d]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
f_{b}
is continuous in
\{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\}
, then
f
is continuous away from grazing set
\{(\eta,\mathfrak{v}):\eta=0,\mathfrak{v}_{1}=0\}
.

Remark 1.2
.

We also remark that the restriction
\mathfrak{a}<\frac{2}{3}
is due to some technical requirement in the proof. With the help of Theorem
1.1
, we can construct the Knudsen boundary layers
\hat{F}_{k}(t,\eta,\phi,\mathfrak{v})
with
\eta\in(0,d)
or equivalently
r\in(1-\varepsilon^{2-\mathfrak{a}},1)
. That means the Knudsen layers are only defined in domain near the boundary of disk. For later use, we introduce a truncation so that it is meaningful in the whole disk. In fact, we define

(1.39)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathscr{F}}_{k}=\Upsilon(\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{a}}\eta)\hat{F}_{k},

and denote
\hat{\mathscr{F}}^{\varepsilon}\sim\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\varepsilon^{k}\hat{\mathscr{F}}_{k}.
Due to the exponential decay on
\eta
, the error generated from the truncation has a very high order
\varepsilon
-decay rate, and it is enough for us to close remainder estimate.

Remark 1.3
.

Based on our above constructions, we hope that the specular reflection boundary condition holds for each order, that is

\displaystyle(F_{k}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}+\hat{F}_{k})(0,\phi,\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2})|_{\mathfrak{v}_{1}>0}=(F_{k}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{k}+\hat{F}_{k})(0,\phi,-\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2}),\quad k\geq 1,

which implies the form of boundary condition
\eqref{K}_{2}
.

Remark 1.4
.

The construction of Knudsen layer and Hilbert expansion requires the estimate for the derivative
\partial_{t}^{l}\hat{F}_{k}
,
\partial_{\phi}^{j}\hat{F}_{k}
,
l,j\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. See (
1.31
) and (
1.43
) for details. Taking
\partial_{t}^{l}
and
\partial_{\phi}^{j}
relatively to (
1.32
), since we separate out the real tangential term by considering geometric correction, we get the equations of
\partial_{t}^{l}\hat{f}_{k}
and
\partial_{\phi}^{j}\hat{f}_{k}
which keep the structure as the equation of
\hat{f}_{k}
. Then we apply Theorem
1.1
to obtain the estimates for
\partial_{t}^{l}\hat{f}_{k}
and
\partial_{\phi}^{j}\hat{f}_{k}
.

1.3.
Truncated Hilbert expansion and main result

Motivated by
[
32
,
54
]
, we consider the Boltzmann solution of (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) in the following Hilbert expansion with multi-scales

\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=\mu(t,x,v)+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{i}F_{i}(t,x,v)+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{i}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}(t,\phi,y,v)

(1.40)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{i}\hat{\mathscr{F}}_{i}(t,\phi,\eta,\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2})+\varepsilon^{5}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v),

which, noting the constructions of
F_{i},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i},\hat{\mathscr{F}}_{i}
in section
1.2
, yields that

(1.41)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}[Q(F_{R}^{\varepsilon},\mu)+Q(\mu,F_{R}^{\varepsilon})]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{3}Q(F_{R}^{\varepsilon},F_{R}^{\varepsilon})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{i-2}\{Q(F_{i}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}+\hat{\mathscr{F}}_{i},F_{R}^{\varepsilon})+Q(F_{R}^{\varepsilon},F_{i}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}+\hat{\mathscr{F}}_{i})\}

\displaystyle\quad+R^{\varepsilon}+\bar{R}^{\varepsilon}+\hat{R}^{\varepsilon},

where

\displaystyle R^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{N-6}\{\partial_{t}F_{N-1}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{N-1}+\varepsilon\partial_{t}F_{N}+\varepsilon v\cdot\nabla_{x}F_{N}\}+\varepsilon^{N-6}\sum\limits_{1\leq i,j\leq N\atop i+j\geq N+1}\varepsilon^{i+j-N-1}Q(F_{i},F_{j}),

\displaystyle\bar{R}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{N-6}\left\{\partial_{t}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{N-1}-\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{\tau})\partial_{\phi}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{N-1}+\varepsilon\partial_{t}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{N}-\varepsilon\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{\tau})\partial_{\phi}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{N}+(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{n})\partial_{y}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{N}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{N-6}\sum\limits_{1\leq l,j\leq N\atop l+j\geq N+1}\varepsilon^{l+j-N-1}\frac{(-y)l}{l!}[Q(\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0})]

(1.42)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{N-6}\sum\limits_{1\leq l,j\leq N\atop l+j\geq N+1}\varepsilon^{l+j-N-1}[Q(F_{l}^{0},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},F_{l}^{0})]

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{N-6}\sum\limits_{1\leq i,l,j\leq N\atop i+l+j\geq N+1}\varepsilon^{i+l+j-N-1}\frac{(-y)^{l}}{l!}[Q(\partial_{r}^{l}F_{i}^{0},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}F_{i}^{0})]

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{N-6}\sum\limits_{1\leq i,j\leq N\atop i+j\geq N+1}\varepsilon^{i+j-N-1}[Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i})]

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{b}-5}\frac{(-y)^{\mathfrak{b}+1}}{(\mathfrak{b}+1)!}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{j-1}[Q(\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\tilde{\mu},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\tilde{\mu})]

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{b}-4}\frac{(-y)^{\mathfrak{b}+1}}{(\mathfrak{b}+1)!}\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{i+j-2}[Q(\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\mathcal{F}_{i},\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j})+Q(\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{j},\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\mathcal{F}_{i})]

\displaystyle\quad-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{i-5}\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon y}(1-\Upsilon(\varepsilon y))(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{\tau})\partial_{\phi}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i},

and

\displaystyle\hat{R}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{N-6}\Upsilon(\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{a}}\eta)\left(\partial_{t}\hat{F}_{N-1}-\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\partial_{\phi}\hat{F}_{N-1}+\varepsilon\partial_{t}\hat{F}_{N}-\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\partial_{\phi}\hat{F}_{N}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{N-6}\Upsilon(\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{a}}\eta)\sum\limits_{1\leq l,j\leq N\atop 2l+j\geq N+1}\varepsilon^{2l+j-N-1}\frac{(-\eta)^{l}}{l!}[Q(\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}\mu_{0})]

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{N-6}\Upsilon(\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{a}}\eta)\sum\limits_{1\leq i,j,l\leq N\atop 2l+i+j\geq N+1}\varepsilon^{2l+i+j-N-1}\frac{(-\eta)^{l}}{l!}[Q(\partial_{r}^{l}F_{i}^{0},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},\partial_{r}^{l}F_{i}^{0})]

(1.43)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{N-6}\Upsilon(\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{a}}\eta)\sum\limits_{1\leq i,j\leq N\atop i+j\geq N+1}\varepsilon^{i+j-N-1}[Q(F_{i}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F_{j}},F_{i}+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i})+Q(\hat{F}_{i},\hat{F}_{j})]

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{N-6}\Upsilon(\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{a}}\eta)\sum\limits_{1\leq i,j,l\leq N\atop i+j+l\geq N+1}\varepsilon^{i+j+l-N-1}\frac{\eta^{l}}{l!}[Q(\partial_{y}^{l}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}^{0},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},\partial_{y}^{l}\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}^{0})]

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{2\mathfrak{b}-4}\Upsilon(\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{a}}\eta)\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{j-1}\frac{(-\eta)^{\mathfrak{b}+1}}{(\mathfrak{b}+1)!}[Q(\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\tilde{\mu},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\tilde{\mu})]

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{2\mathfrak{b}-3}\Upsilon(\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{a}}\eta)\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{i+j-2}\frac{(-\eta)^{\mathfrak{b}+1}}{(\mathfrak{b}+1)!}[Q(\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\mathcal{F}_{i},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},\partial_{r}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\mathcal{F}_{i})]

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{b}-4}\Upsilon(\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{a}}\eta)\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{i+j-2}\frac{\eta^{\mathfrak{b}+1}}{(\mathfrak{b}+1)!}[Q(\partial_{y}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{i},\hat{F}_{j})+Q(\hat{F}_{j},\partial_{y}^{\mathfrak{b}+1}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{i})]

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{i-7+\mathfrak{a}}(\mathfrak{v}_{1}\partial_{z}\Upsilon(z))\hat{F}_{k}.

For later use, we define

\tilde{w}_{\mathbf{k}}:=(1+|v|^{2})^{\frac{\mathbf{k}}{2}}
with
\mathbf{k}
to be determined. Our main result is

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
\mathbf{k}\geq 6,N\geq 6
and
\mathfrak{b}\geq 5
. We assume the initial data

\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}(0,x,v)=

\displaystyle\mu(0,x,v)+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{i}\{F_{i}(0,x,v)+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}(0,y,\phi,\bar{v})+\hat{\mathscr{F}}_{i}(0,\eta,\phi,\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2})\}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{5}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}(0)\geq 0,

and
F_{i}(0),\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}(0),\hat{\mathscr{F}}_{i}(0),i=0,1,...,N
satisfy the regularity and compatibility condition described in Proposition
5.1
, and

\displaystyle\|(\frac{F_{R}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\mu}})(0)\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}+\varepsilon^{3}\|(\tilde{w}_{\mathbf{k}}\frac{F_{R}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}})(0)\|_{L_{x,v}^{\infty}}\textless\infty.

Then there exists a small positive constant
\varepsilon_{0}\textgreater 0
such that equation (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) has a unique solution for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
over the time interval
t\in[0,\tau^{\delta}]
in the following form of expansion

\displaystyle F^{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)=

\displaystyle\mu(t,x,v)+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{i}\{F_{i}(t,x,v)+\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}(t,\phi,\frac{1-r}{\varepsilon},\bar{v})+\hat{\mathscr{F}}_{i}(t,\phi,\frac{1-r}{\varepsilon^{2}},\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2})\}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{5}F_{R}^{\varepsilon}\geq 0,

with

(1.44)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\tau^{\delta}]}\Big(\|(\frac{F_{R}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\mu}})(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}}+\varepsilon^{3}\|(\tilde{w}_{\mathbf{k}}\frac{F_{R}^{\varepsilon}}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}})(t)\|_{L_{x,v}^{\infty}}\Big)\leq C(\tau^{\delta})\textless\infty,

where
\tau^{\delta}>0
is the life-span of smooth solution of compressible Euler equation (
1.9
)–(
1.11
). Here the functions
F_{i}(t,x,v),\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}(t,\phi,y,v),\hat{\mathscr{F}}_{i}(t,\phi,\eta,\mathfrak{v})
are respectively the internal expansion, viscous and Knudsen boundary layers constructed in Section
5.1
.

Remark 1.6
.

From (
1.44
) and Section
5.1
, we have

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{t\in[0,\tau^{\delta}]}\left\{\left\|\left(\frac{F^{\varepsilon}-\mu}{\sqrt{\mu}}\right)(t)\right\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\left\|\tilde{w}_{\mathbf{k}}\left(\frac{F^{\varepsilon}-\mu}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}\right)(t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})}\right\}\leq C\varepsilon\to 0.

That means we have justified the validity of hydrodynamic limit from the Boltzmann equation to the compressible Euler system for the specular boundary problem in 2D disk.

1.4.
Key points of the proof

We now make some comments on the key points of this paper.

In the disk, due to the coexistence of nonzero boundary curvature and nonzero tangential velocity
u_{\tau}^{0}\neq 0
at the boundary, it is highly nontrivial to construct the solution for geometrically modified Knudsen layers.
On the other hand, to control the remainder term, we would like to use the
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
arguments in
[
27
]
where it is important to remove some singular region with small measure (it may depend on
\varepsilon
) so that the change of variable can be applied. Since the backward characteristics are complex, it is not an easy job to describe the
\varepsilon
-dependent singular region.
In this paper, by using the polar coordinate, we give an explicit description for the backward characteristic of the remainder, revealing finite collision times under
|\bar{\mathfrak{v}}|\leq N,\ |\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}/\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}|\geq\bar{m}
, which facilitates removing the singular part.
We explain the key ideas of our proof in the following.

1.4.1.
The existence of Knudsen layer with geometric correction and nonzero
u^{0}_{\tau}\neq 0
.

We consider the following boundary value equation

(1.45)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}f=S,\\
\displaystyle f(0,\mathfrak{v})|_{\gamma_{-}}=f\left(0,-\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2}\right),\\
\displaystyle f(d,\mathfrak{v})|_{\gamma_{-}}=0.\end{cases}

where

(1.46)

\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}f:=\mathfrak{v}_{1}\partial_{\eta}f+G(\eta)\left(\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{1}}-\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{2}}\right)+(1-\lambda)\nu f+\lambda\mathbf{L}_{0}f-\frac{u_{\tau}^{0}}{2T^{0}}G(\eta)\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}f.

In the following, we shall denote the solution of (
1.45
) as
f_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}^{-1}S
. We aim to establish the existence of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}^{-1}S
, especially
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{-1}S
which is in fact our original Knudsen boundary layer problem (
1.33
).
Here we assume zero incoming boundary at
\eta=d
, which is essential for us to derive the exponential decay of
\eta
.

Step 1.
To construct
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{-1}S
, we first consider the existence of
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{-1}S
. It is a standard approach to consider the following approximate BVP:

(1.47)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}_{0}f=S,\\
f(0,\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2})|_{\mathfrak{v}_{1}>0}=(1-\frac{1}{n})f(0,-\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2}),\\
f(d,\mathfrak{v})|_{\mathfrak{v}_{1}<0}=0.\end{cases}

We denote the solution of (
1.47
) as
f^{n}
and define
\displaystyle\mathbf{f}^{n}=\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}f^{n}
. In fact, for given
n\geq 2
, we can obtain the solution
f^{n}
relatively easy by an iterative arguments. The coefficient plays an important role in the construction of solution
f^{n}
, see steps 1 & 2 in the proof of Lemma
4.3
.

Then we consider the limit
n\to\infty
and it is better to use the equation of
\mathbf{f}^{n}
. For
n_{1}>1,n_{2}>1
, it holds that

(1.48)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}_{1}\partial_{\eta}(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}})+G(\eta)\left(\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\frac{\partial\left(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}}\right)}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{1}}-\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}\frac{\partial\left(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}}\right)}{\partial\mathfrak{v}_{2}}\right)+\nu\left(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}}\right)=0,\\
\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}}\right)(0,\mathfrak{v})|_{\gamma_{-}}=\left(1-\frac{1}{n_{1}}\right)\left(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}}\right)\left(0,-\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{n_{2}}-\frac{1}{n_{1}}\right)\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}}\left(0,-\mathfrak{v}_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{2}\right),\\
\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}}\right)(d,\mathfrak{v})|_{\gamma_{-}}=0.\end{cases}

Along its backward characteristic in (
4.8
), one has

\displaystyle w_{\beta}(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}})(\eta,\mathfrak{v})=

\displaystyle(1-\frac{1}{n_{1}})^{k}w_{\beta}(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}})(0,v_{k})e^{-\nu(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}_{k})}

(1.49)

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=1}^{k}(1-\frac{1}{n_{1}})^{l-1}(\frac{1}{n_{2}}-\frac{1}{n_{1}})w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}}(0,v_{l})e^{-\nu(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}_{l})}.

Due to the geometric correction in (
1.46
), its backward characteristics are very complex which brings difficulty in the
L^{\infty}
estimate of (
1.49
).
In fact, the particles may oscillate in
[0,\eta_{+}]
where
\eta_{+}
is the one defined in (
4.10
) (see also (
4.26
) for more detail).

It is noted that
\eta_{+}
may approach zero if
\eta\ll 1
and
|\mathfrak{v}_{1}/\mathfrak{v}_{2}|\ll 1
. Thus, we have to take the number of collision
k
sufficiently large so that
\displaystyle e^{-\nu(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}_{k})}
will provide a small coefficient for the first term on RHS of (
1.49
). However, for
k\gg 1
, difficulty arises in the second term on RHS of (
1.49
). Fortunately, by noting the normal velocity
V_{cl,1}(X(s))
decreases with
X(s)
, then it holds that
|v_{k,1}|\geq\tilde{m}
if
|\mathfrak{v}_{1}|\geq\tilde{m}
. Also, we find that
(1+|\mathfrak{v}|^{2})\eta_{+}
has a lower bound if
|\mathfrak{v}_{1}|\geq\tilde{m}
. Thus by choosing
k=(1+|\mathfrak{v}|^{2})k_{0}(\tilde{m})
with
k_{0}(\tilde{m})\gg 1
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathbf{1}_{\{|\mathfrak{v}_{1}|\geq\tilde{m}\}}w_{\beta}(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}})(0,v_{k})|e^{-\nu(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}_{k})}

\displaystyle\leq|\mathbf{1}_{\{|\mathfrak{v}_{1}|\geq\tilde{m}\}}w_{\beta}(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}})|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma_{+})}e^{-2(k-1)\eta_{+}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{8}|\mathbf{1}_{\{|\mathfrak{v}_{1}|\geq\tilde{m}\}}w_{\beta}(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}})|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma_{+})},

which yields that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{1}_{\{|\mathfrak{v}_{1}|\geq\tilde{m}\}}w_{\beta}(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}+|\mathbf{1}_{\{|\mathfrak{v}_{1}|\geq\tilde{m}\}}w_{\beta}(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}})|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma_{+})}

(1.50)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\tilde{m}}(\frac{1}{n_{1}}+\frac{1}{n_{2}})|w_{\beta+2}\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}}|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma_{+})}\leq C_{\tilde{m}}(\frac{1}{n_{1}}+\frac{1}{n_{2}})\|\nu^{-1}w_{\beta+2}\bar{S}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}\,\to 0,

as
n_{1},n_{2}\to\infty
.
Noting
\tilde{m}
can be any positive constant, we get the limit function
\mathbf{f}_{0}
and the continuity of
\mathbf{f}_{0}
in
[0,d]\times\{\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash{\mathfrak{v}_{1}=0}\}
.

Next we consider the limit at
(0,d]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. It is better to rewrite (
1.49
) in the following form

\displaystyle w_{\beta}(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}})(\eta,\mathfrak{v})

\displaystyle=(1-\frac{1}{n})^{k}w_{\beta}(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}})(\eta,V_{cl}(\mathfrak{t}_{c}))e^{-\nu(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}_{k})-\nu(\mathfrak{t}_{k}-\mathfrak{t}_{c})}

(1.51)

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=1}^{k}(1-\frac{1}{n})^{l-1}w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}}(0,v_{l})e^{-\nu(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}_{l})},

where we have denoted
\mathfrak{t}_{c}\in(\mathfrak{t}_{k+1},\mathfrak{t}_{k})
to be the time so that the backward characteristic moves from
X(\mathfrak{t}_{k})=0
to
X(\mathfrak{t}_{c})=\eta
.
For
\eta\geq\tilde{m}^{\prime}
, it is key to note that
\eta_{+}\geq\eta\geq\tilde{m}^{\prime}
, then we choose
k=k_{0}^{\prime}(\tilde{m}^{\prime})
with
k_{0}^{\prime}(\tilde{m}^{\prime})\gg 1
such that

\displaystyle e^{-\nu(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}_{k})}\leq e^{-(k-1)\int_{\mathfrak{t}_{2}}^{\mathfrak{t}_{1}}|V_{cl,1}|\,d\tau}=e^{-2(k-1)\eta_{+}}\leq\frac{1}{8}.

By some detailed analysis, one obtains from (
1.51
) that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{1}_{\{\eta\geq\tilde{m}^{\prime}\}}w_{\beta}(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{8}\|\mathbf{1}_{\{\eta\geq\tilde{m}^{\prime}\}}w_{\beta}(\mathbf{f}^{n_{1}}-\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}})\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}+C_{\tilde{m}^{\prime}}(\frac{1}{n_{1}}+\frac{1}{n_{2}})|w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}}|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma_{+})}

(1.52)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\tilde{m}^{\prime}}(\frac{1}{n_{1}}+\frac{1}{n_{2}})|w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}^{n_{2}}|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma_{+})}\,\to 0,\quad n_{1},n_{2}\to\infty.

It follows from (
1.50
) and (
1.52
) that
w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}^{n}\to w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}_{0}
in
L^{\infty}(\tilde{m}^{\prime},d]\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
w_{\beta-2}\mathbf{f}^{n}\to w_{\beta-2}\mathbf{f}_{0}
in
L^{\infty}([0,d]\times\{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|\mathfrak{v}_{1}|\geq\tilde{m}\})
. Since
\tilde{m},\tilde{m}^{\prime}
can be arbitrary small, it is clear to know that
w_{\beta-2}\mathbf{f}^{n}\to w_{\beta-2}\mathbf{f}_{0}
a.e. in
\Omega_{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
and also
f^{n}-f_{0}\to 0
in
L^{2}(\Omega_{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
f_{0}=\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}\mathbf{f}_{0}
.

Finally, due to the arbitrary smallness of
\tilde{m},\tilde{m}^{\prime}
and the continuity of
\mathbf{f}^{n}
, by noting (
1.50
) and (
1.52
), it is clear to see that
\mathbf{f}_{0}
(or
f_{0}
) is continuous away from grazing set
\{(\eta,\mathfrak{v}):\eta=0,\mathfrak{v}_{1}=0\}
.

Step 2.
To extend
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{-1}S
to
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{-1}S
, we need some
L^{2}
and
L^{\infty}
uniform-in-
\lambda
estimates for
\lambda\in[0,1]
.

Step 2.1. The a priori
L^{2}
-estimate.
We point out that it is almost impossible to control
\displaystyle G(\eta)\frac{u_{\tau}^{0}}{2T^{0}}\mathfrak{v}_{1}\mathfrak{v}_{2}f_{\lambda}
in pure
L^{2}
-estimate. By noting the smallness of
G(\eta)\sim\varepsilon^{2}
for
\eta\in[0,d]
with
d=\varepsilon^{-\mathfrak{a}}
, the key idea is to control it by weighted
L^{\infty}
-norm. In fact, multiplying (
1.45
) by
f_{\lambda}
, one has

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}|f_{\lambda}(d)|_{L^{2}(\gamma_{+})}^{2}+(1-\lambda)\int_{0}^{d}\|f_{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}_{\mathfrak{v}}}^{2}\,d\eta+\lambda c_{0}\int_{0}^{d}\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{0})f_{\lambda}\|_{\nu}^{2}\,d\eta

(1.53)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2-\mathfrak{a}}\|w\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}^{2}+C\int_{0}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|f_{\lambda}\cdot S|\,d\mathfrak{v}\,d\eta,

where the small coefficient
\varepsilon^{2-\mathfrak{a}}
plays an important role.

To derive uniform-in-
\lambda

L^{2}
-estimate, we divide the discussion into two cases:

Case 1.
\lambda\in[0,1-\varepsilon]
.
It is clear that
1-\lambda\geq\varepsilon
. By utilizing the smallness of
\varepsilon
, we directly have from (
1.53
) that

(1.54)

\displaystyle\left\|f_{\lambda}\right\|_{L_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}^{2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\mathfrak{a})}\left\|w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}\right\|_{L_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}^{\infty}}+C_{d}\|S\|_{L_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}^{2}}.

Case 2.
\lambda\in[1-\varepsilon,1]
.
Since
\lambda\geq 1-\varepsilon
, we shall utilize the good term
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{d}\|(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{0})f_{\lambda}\|_{\nu}^{2}\,d\eta
to derive the desired
L^{2}
estimate. In fact, by taking some suitable test functions as in
[
24
,
33
]
, we can control the macroscopic part
\|\mathbf{P}_{0}f_{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}}
by the microscopic part, and then we obtain

(1.55)

\displaystyle\|f_{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}(\min\{2-3\mathfrak{a},1-\mathfrak{a}\})}\|w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}+C_{\varepsilon,d}\|S\|_{L^{2}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}.

We refer to Lemma
4.4
for details.

Step 2.2. The a priori
L^{\infty}
-estimate.
We shall use
\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}\equiv\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}{\sqrt{\mu_{M}}}f_{\lambda}
to consider the
L^{\infty}
-estimate. By some detailed analysis, we have

\displaystyle\|w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}+|w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma_{+})}\leq C\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{2\zeta|\mathfrak{v}|^{2}}|\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}|^{2}(\eta,\mathfrak{v})d\mathfrak{v}\Big|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta}}+C\|\nu^{-1}w_{\beta}\bar{S}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}.

Then we need to estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{2\zeta|\mathfrak{v}|^{2}}|\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}|^{2}(\eta,\mathfrak{v})d\mathfrak{v}
. Noting the mild formulation of
\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}
in (
4.60
)–(
4.62
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{2\zeta|\mathfrak{v}|^{2}}|\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}|^{2}(\eta,\mathfrak{v})d\mathfrak{v}\lesssim o(1)\big(\|w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}+|w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma_{+})}\big)^{2}+\|\nu^{-1}w_{\beta}\bar{S}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}

(1.56)

\displaystyle+\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\{|\mathfrak{v}|\leq\mathfrak{N},|\mathfrak{v}_{1}|\geq m,|\mathfrak{v}_{1}/\mathfrak{v}_{2}|\geq m\}}e^{2\zeta|\mathfrak{v}|^{2}}\left\{\int_{\mathfrak{t}_{k}}^{\mathfrak{t}}e^{-\nu(\mathfrak{v})(\mathfrak{t}-s)}|K_{M}\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}|(X_{cl}(s),V_{cl}(s))ds\right\}^{2}\,d\mathfrak{v},

where we have removed some singular region with small measure. We remark that
o(1)
on RHS of (
1.56
) depends on the smallness of such singular region, and
k>0
is a number depending on
m^{-1}
.

To control the last term on RHS of (
1.56
), we shall use the change of variables
dsd\mathfrak{v}\to dXdV
where we denote
(X_{cl},V_{cl})
as
(X,V)
. It is a challenging task to directly calculate the Jacobian determinant

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(X,V)}{\partial(s,\mathfrak{v})}
, and thus it is advisable to compute the Jacobian in a stepwise manner. We note

\displaystyle X(s)=\eta_{l}-\int_{s}^{\mathfrak{t}_{l}}|V_{cl,1}(\tau)|d\tau,\quad s\in(\mathfrak{t}_{l+1},\mathfrak{t}_{l}].

Since
\eta_{l}=0
or
d
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{dX(s)}{ds}\right|=|V_{cl,1}(s)|=\sqrt{|\mathfrak{v}|^{2}-\mathfrak{v}_{2}^{2}\left(e^{-2W(\eta)+2W(X(s))}\right)}.

Then, in
\{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|\mathfrak{v}|\leq\mathfrak{N},|\mathfrak{v}_{1}|\geq m,|\mathfrak{v}_{1}/\mathfrak{v}_{2}|\geq m\}
, we obtain from above formula that

(1.57)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{dX(s)}{ds}\right|^{2}=|V_{cl,1}|^{2}\geq m^{2}+|\mathfrak{v}_{2}|^{2}\left\{1-\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon^{2}\eta}{1-\varepsilon^{2}X(s)}\right)^{2}\right\}\geq m^{2}-\mathfrak{N}^{2}\varepsilon^{2-\mathfrak{a}}\geq\frac{m^{2}}{4},

where the smallness of
\varepsilon>0
is used.

Using (
1.57
), we first perform the change of variable
ds\to dX
and express
V_{cl}
as a function of
X
and
\mathfrak{v}
, i.e.,
V_{cl}=V_{cl}(X,\mathfrak{v})
. Now it can be checked that
d\mathfrak{v}\sim dV
.

Combining above estimates, we can establish

(1.58)

\displaystyle\|w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}+|w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma_{+})}\leq C\|f_{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}+C\|\nu^{-1}w_{\beta}\bar{S}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}.

We remark that the constant on RHS of (
1.58
) is independent of
d>0
. We refer to Lemma
4.5
for details of proof in
Step 2.2
.

Step 2.3.
From
Steps 2.1
&
2.2
, we can obtain the uniform-in-
\lambda

a priori
estimate

(1.59)

\displaystyle\|w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}+|w_{\beta}\mathbf{f}_{\lambda}|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma_{+})}\leq C_{\varepsilon,d}\|\nu^{-1}w_{\beta}\bar{S}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}.

Then, by the continuity arguments, we can extend the existence result and energy estimate from
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{-1}
to
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{-1}
, see Lemma
4.6
for details.

Step 3. Exponential decay of Knudsen layer solutions.
For the solution
f=\mathcal{L}_{1}^{-1}S
obtained in Step 2,
using the zero incoming boundary condition at
\eta=d
and the special test functions in
[
24
,
33
]
, we obtain the exponential decay estimate

(1.60)

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma\eta}f\|_{L^{2}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}+e^{\sigma d}|f(d)|_{L^{2}(\gamma_{+})}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{\sigma}}\varepsilon^{1-\frac{\mathfrak{a}}{2}}\|e^{\sigma\eta}w\mathbf{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}+\frac{C}{\sqrt{\sigma(\sigma_{2}-\sigma)}}\|e^{\sigma_{2}\eta}S\|_{L^{2}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}},

which yields immediately that

(1.61)

\displaystyle\|e^{\sigma\eta}w\mathbf{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}}+|e^{\sigma\eta}w\mathbf{f}|_{L^{\infty}(\gamma_{+})}\leq\frac{C}{\sigma(\sigma_{0}-\sigma)}\|e^{\sigma_{0}\eta}\nu^{-1}w\bar{S}\|_{L^{\infty}_{\eta,\mathfrak{v}}},

see Section 4.2 for details.
We point out that above decay estimate is crucial for us to close the estimation of remainder of Hilbert expansion.

1.4.2.
The remainder estimate.

To control the remainder term, since the collisions are very complicate, we use the polar coordinate to study its backward characteristics, see (
5.16
). By tedious calculations, we derive

(1.62)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\left|\frac{\partial(\mathcal{X},\Phi)}{\partial\bar{\mathfrak{v}}}(s)\right|=

\displaystyle\frac{|\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}|}{|\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}|}(s-t_{1,0})(t-s)\cdot\left|\frac{2}{\sqrt{\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{|\bar{\mathfrak{v}}|^{2}}{|\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}|^{2}}-1}}-\frac{|\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}|}{|\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}|}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\frac{\mathcal{X}^{2}}{r^{2}}\frac{|\bar{\mathfrak{v}}|^{2}}{|\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}|^{2}}-1}}\right|.

See Lemma
5.4
for details.

With the help of (
1.62
), we divide the
L^{2}-L^{\infty}
estimate into several cases, and remove some regions with small measure which is singular for (
1.62
).
Then we need only to consider the change of variable in the domain

\displaystyle\Big\{(s,\bar{\mathfrak{v}},v^{\prime},\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle\in[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,1]:\,s-t_{1,0}\geq m_{1},t-s\geq m_{1},

(1.63)

\displaystyle|\bar{\mathfrak{v}}|\leq\mathfrak{N},|v^{\prime}|\leq 2\mathfrak{N},|\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{1}/\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}|\geq\bar{m},|\bar{\mathfrak{v}}_{2}|\geq\bar{m},\,\text{and}\quad|\frac{\mathcal{X}}{r}-1|\geq m_{2}\Big\}.

Under (
1.63
), it holds that

(1.64)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\left|\frac{\partial(\mathcal{X},\Phi)}{\partial\bar{\mathfrak{v}}}(s)\right|\geq\frac{m_{2}}{2}m_{1}^{2}.

Then, by the change of variables
d\mathfrak{v}\to\mathcal{X}d\mathcal{X}d\Phi
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\|h^{\varepsilon}_{R}(s)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle C(\|h^{\varepsilon}_{R}(0)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+1)+\left\{\frac{1}{\mathfrak{N}}+C\left(\bar{m}\mathfrak{N}+Ce^{-\frac{1}{3\varepsilon^{2}}\bar{m}^{2}}\right)\right\}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\|h^{\varepsilon}_{R}(s)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left(Cm_{1}^{2}+\frac{C\mathfrak{N}t}{\bar{m}^{6}}m_{2}^{2}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\|h^{\varepsilon}_{R}(s)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\frac{C_{\mathfrak{N}}}{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\|f^{\varepsilon}_{R}(s)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Choosing
\bar{m}=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{N}^{2}}
with
\mathfrak{N}>1
suitably large, and
m_{1}=\bar{m}\varepsilon^{2},m_{2}=\bar{m}^{4}\varepsilon^{2}
, one obtains that

(1.65)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\|\varepsilon^{3}h^{\varepsilon}_{R}(s)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C(t)\{\|\varepsilon^{3}h^{\varepsilon}_{R}(0)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\varepsilon^{N-1}+\varepsilon^{\mathfrak{b}}\}+C\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\|f^{\varepsilon}_{R}(s)\|_{L^{2}},

which, together with the
L^{2}
energy estimate, yields that

(1.66)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}(\varepsilon^{3}\|h_{R}^{\varepsilon}(s)\|_{L^{\infty}})\leq C(t).

See Section 5.2 for more details.

The paper is organized as follows:
In Section
2
, we reformulate the interior, viscous and Knudsen boundary layers. Also we derive the corresponding boundary conditions so that the formulations of interior expansion, the viscous and Knudsen boundary layers are all well-posed. Section
3
is devoted to existence theories for a linear hyperbolic system with characteristic boundary and a linear parabolic system with degenerate viscosity, which are used for the construction of
F_{i}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{F}}_{i}
. Section
4
provides a complete proof of Theorem
1.1
, where we establish the existence of Knudsen boundary layer problem and obtain spatial exponential decay. In Section
5
, we construct each layer and control the remainder estimate, which completes the proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Notations.
Throughout this paper,
C>0
denotes a constant. It is
referred to as universal and can change from one inequality to another. When we write
C(z)
or
C_{z}
, it means a certain positive constant depending on the quantity
z
. We use
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
to denote the standard
L^{2}
inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{v}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\nu}=\|\sqrt{\nu}\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{v}}.

\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}}
denotes the standard
L^{2}(B_{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{v})
-norm, and
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}}
denotes the
L^{\infty}(B_{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{v})
-norm.

2.
Reformulation and boundary conditions

Firstly we introduce the existence result on the compressible Euler equations.

Lemma 2.1

(
[
52
]
)
.

Let
s_{0}\geq 2
be some positive number. Consider the IBVP of compressible Euler equations (
1.9
)–(
1.11
). Choose
\delta\in(0,\delta_{1}]
so that for any
\delta\in(0,\delta_{1}],
the positivity of
\rho_{0}
and
T_{0}
is guaranteed. Then for
\delta\in(0,\delta_{1}]
, there is a family of classical solutions
(\rho^{\delta},\mathfrak{u}^{\delta},T^{\delta})\in C([0,\tau^{\delta}];H^{s_{0}}(B_{1}))\cap C^{1}([0,\tau^{\delta}];H^{s_{0}-1}(B_{1}))
of IBVP (
1.9
)–(
1.11
) such that
\rho^{\delta}>0
and
T^{\delta}>0
, and the following estimate holds:

(2.1)

\displaystyle\|(\rho^{\delta}-1,\mathfrak{u}^{\delta},T^{\delta}-1)\|_{C([0,\tau^{\delta}];H^{s_{0}-}(B_{1}))\cap C^{1}([0,\tau^{\delta}];H^{s_{0}-1-}(B_{1}))}\leq C_{0}.

The constant
C_{0}
is independent of
\delta
, depending only on the
H^{s_{0}}
norm of
(\phi_{0},\Phi_{0},\vartheta_{0})
.

We refer
[
52
]
for the local existence of the IBVP of compressible Euler equation (
1.9
)-(
1.11
), see also
[
10
]
and the references cited therein.

Remark 2.2
.

By the analysis in Section 3.1, we know that the following holds for 2D-disk problem:

(2.2)

\displaystyle\|(\rho^{\delta}-1,\mathfrak{u}^{\delta},T^{\delta}-1)\|_{C([0,\tau^{\delta}];H^{s_{0}}(B_{1}))\cap C^{1}([0,\tau^{\delta}];H^{s_{0}-1}(B_{1}))}\leq C_{0}.

2.1.
Reformulation of internal solutions

We introduce the linear hyperbolic systems for the macroscopic variables
(\rho_{k+1},u_{k+1},\theta_{k+1})
of internal solutions
f_{k+1}
.

Lemma 2.3

(
[
32
]
)
.

Let
(\rho,\mathfrak{u},T)(t)
be some smooth solution of compressible Euler equation (
1.9
)–(
1.11
). For each given non-negative integer
k
, assume
F_{k}
is found. Then the macroscopic part
(\rho_{k+1},u_{k+1},\theta_{k+1})
satisfy the following:

(2.3)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho_{k+1}+\text{\rm div}(\rho u_{k+1}+\rho_{k+1}\mathfrak{u})=0,\\
\displaystyle\rho\{\partial_{t}u_{k+1}+(u_{k+1}\cdot\nabla_{x})\mathfrak{u}+(\mathfrak{u}\cdot\nabla_{x})u_{k+1}\}-\frac{\rho_{k+1}}{\rho}\nabla(\rho T)+\nabla_{x}\left(\frac{\rho\theta_{k+1}+2\rho_{k+1}T}{2}\right)=h_{k},\\
\displaystyle\rho\{\partial_{t}\theta_{k+1}+(\theta_{k+1}\text{\rm div}u+2T\text{\rm div}u_{k+1})+\mathfrak{u}\cdot\nabla_{x}\theta_{k+1}+2u_{k+1}\cdot\nabla_{x}T\}=g_{k},\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle h_{k,i}

\displaystyle=-\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}\partial_{x_{j}}\left(T\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left[\frac{(v_{i}-\mathfrak{u}_{i})(v_{j}-\mathfrak{u}_{j})}{T}-\frac{1}{2T}|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}\delta_{ij}\right]F_{k+1}dv\right),

\displaystyle g_{k}

\displaystyle=-\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}\partial_{x_{j}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(v_{j}-\mathfrak{u}_{j})(|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}-4T)F_{k+1}dv+2\mathfrak{u}_{i}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}[(v_{i}-\mathfrak{u}_{i})(v_{j}-\mathfrak{u}_{j})\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\left.\frac{1}{2}|v-\mathfrak{u}|^{2}\delta_{ij}]F_{k+1}dv\right\}+2\mathfrak{u}\cdot h_{k}.

We have used the subscript
k
for the source terms
h_{k}
and
g_{k}
in order to emphasize that the right hand side depends only on
F_{i}
’s for
0\leq i\leq k
.

The detailed calculations leading to (
2.3
) are omitted here for brevity; we refer readers to
[
32
]
for a comprehensive derivation. The microscopic part
(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P})f_{k+1}
can be determined by (
1.15
).

Remark 2.4
.

To solve (
1.8
), the problem equivalently reduces to solving the linear hyperbolic system (
2.3
). As an initial-boundary value problem, proper boundary condition must be specified for (
2.3
). However, the boundary data cannot be arbitrarily assigned: to ensure that each component
F_{k}+\bar{F}_{k}+\hat{F}_{k}
satisfies the specular reflection boundary condition and guarantees solvability of the Knudsen layer, careful constraints are required.
Determining the boundary condition constitutes a highly technical procedure, the details of which are systematically addressed in Section 2.4.

Remark 2.5
.

With suitable boundary condition, the existence of smooth solution of hyperbolic equations (
2.3
) can be found in Section 3.1.

2.2.
Reformulation of viscous boundary layers

By
\eqref{vs}_{1}
, we have
\bar{f}_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
, i.e.

(2.4)

\bar{f}_{1}=\mathbf{P}_{0}\bar{f}_{1}=\left\{\frac{\bar{\rho}_{1}}{\rho^{0}}+\bar{u}_{1}\cdot\frac{\bar{v}-\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}}{T^{0}}+\frac{\bar{{\theta}}_{1}}{4T^{0}}(\frac{|\bar{v}-\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}|^{2}}{T^{0}}-2)\right\}\sqrt{\mu_{0}}.

The far-field assumption (
1.21
) becomes

(2.5)

\lim\limits_{y\rightarrow\infty}\bar{f}_{k}(t,y,\phi,\bar{v})=0.

Multiplying
\eqref{vs}_{2}
by
1,(\bar{v}\cdot\vec{n})
and integrating on
v
, one has

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\langle\bar{v}\cdot\vec{n},\bar{F}_{1}\rangle=\partial_{y}(\rho^{0}\bar{u}_{1}\cdot\vec{n})=\rho^{0}\partial_{y}(\bar{u}_{1}\cdot\vec{n})=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\langle|\bar{v}\cdot\vec{n}|^{2},\bar{F}_{1}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{y}(\bar{\rho}_{1}T^{0}+2\rho^{0}\bar{\theta}_{1})=\partial_{y}\bar{p}_{1}=0,

which, together with (
2.5
), yields that

(2.6)

\bar{u}_{1}\cdot\vec{n}\equiv 0,\quad\bar{p}_{1}(t,y,\phi,\bar{v})\equiv 0,\quad\forall(t,y,\phi,\bar{v})\in[0,\tau^{\delta})\times[0,\infty)\times[0,2\pi)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}.

We now formulate the linear parabolic system for
(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{n},\bar{\theta}_{k})
and the equations for
(\bar{u}_{k+1}\cdot\vec{n},\bar{p}_{k+1})
.

Lemma 2.6

(
[
32
]
)
.

The parabolic system on
(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{\tau},\bar{\theta}_{k})
is as following:

(2.7)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\rho^{0}\partial_{t}(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{\tau})-\kappa_{1}(T^{0})\partial_{yy}(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{\tau})\\
\quad-\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\rho^{0}u_{\tau}^{0}\partial_{\phi}(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{\tau})+\rho^{0}(-y\partial_{r}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\cdot\vec{n}-u^{0}_{1}\cdot\vec{n})\partial_{y}(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{\tau})\\
\quad+(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{\tau})\cdot\left\{\left(1-\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\right)u_{\tau}^{0}\partial_{\phi}\rho^{0}+\left(1-2\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\right)\rho^{0}\partial_{\phi}u_{\tau}^{0}\right\}\\
\quad-\frac{\rho^{0}}{2T^{0}}\bar{\theta}_{k}\cdot\left\{\left(1-\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\right)u_{\tau}^{0}\partial_{\phi}u_{\tau}^{0}+\partial_{\phi}T^{0}+\frac{T^{0}}{\rho^{0}}\partial_{\phi}\rho^{0}\right\}=\bar{h}_{k-1},\\
\\
\rho^{0}\partial_{t}\bar{\theta}_{k}-\kappa_{2}(T^{0})\partial_{yy}\bar{\theta}_{k}+\rho^{0}(-y\partial_{r}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\cdot\vec{n}-u_{1}^{0}\cdot\vec{n})\partial_{y}\bar{\theta}_{k}-\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\rho^{0}u_{\tau}^{0}\partial_{\phi}\bar{\theta}_{k}\\
\quad+\bar{\theta}_{k}\left\{\left(1-\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\right)\partial_{\phi}(\rho^{0}u_{\tau}^{0})-\frac{\rho^{0}}{T^{0}}\left(1-\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\right)u_{\tau}^{0}\partial_{\phi}T^{0}-\rho^{0}\partial_{\phi}u_{\tau}^{0}-\rho^{0}\partial_{r}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\cdot\vec{n}\right\}\\
\quad+\rho^{0}(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{\tau})\left\{\left(1-2\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\right)\partial_{\phi}T^{0}+\frac{T^{0}}{\rho^{0}}\partial_{\phi}\rho^{0}+\left(1-\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\right)u_{\tau}^{0}\partial_{\phi}u_{\tau}^{0}\right\}=\bar{g}_{k-1},\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle\bar{h}_{k-1}:=

\displaystyle-\frac{\bar{p}_{k}}{T^{0}}\cdot\left\{\left(1-\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\right)u_{\tau}^{0}\partial_{\phi}u_{\tau}^{0}+\partial_{\phi}T^{0}+\frac{T^{0}}{\rho^{0}}\partial_{\phi}\rho^{0}\right\}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\partial_{\phi}\bar{p}_{k}+\left(\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)u_{\tau}^{0}+\partial_{r}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\cdot\vec{\tau}\right)\rho^{0}(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{n})

\displaystyle-\rho^{0}(-y\partial_{r}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\cdot\vec{\tau}-u^{0}_{1}\cdot\vec{\tau}+\bar{u}_{1}\cdot\vec{\tau})\partial_{y}(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{n})+\bar{W}_{k-1}-T^{0}\partial_{y}\langle\mathcal{A}_{12}^{0},\bar{J}_{k-1}\rangle,

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{k-1}:=

\displaystyle\,\partial_{t}\bar{p}_{k}-\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)u_{\tau}^{0}\partial_{\phi}\bar{p}_{k}-\frac{\bar{p}_{k}}{T^{0}}\left\{\left(2-2\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\right)u_{\tau}^{0}\partial_{\phi}T^{0}+2T^{0}\text{\rm div}\bar{\mathfrak{u}}^{0}\right\}

\displaystyle-\rho^{0}\left\{\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)|u_{\tau}^{0}|^{2}-2\partial_{r}T^{0}\right\}(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{n})-\rho^{0}(-2y\partial_{r}T^{0}+\theta_{1}^{0}+\bar{\theta}_{1})\partial_{y}(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{n})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\bar{H}_{k-1}-\frac{1}{2}(2T^{0})^{\frac{3}{2}}\partial_{y}\langle\mathcal{B}_{1}^{0},\bar{J}_{k-1}\rangle,

and
\bar{W}_{k-1},\bar{H}_{k-1}
,
\bar{J}_{k-1}
and
\kappa_{1}(T^{0}),\kappa_{2}(T^{0})
are defined by (
6.7
), (
6.17
), (
6.9
), (
6.1
) and (
6.2
).

One also has the equations for
\bar{u}_{k+1}\cdot\vec{n}
and
\bar{p}_{k+1}
as

(2.8)

\displaystyle\partial_{y}(\bar{u}_{k+1}\cdot\vec{n})=-\frac{1}{\rho^{0}}\partial_{t}\bar{\rho}_{k}+\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\frac{1}{\rho^{0}}\partial_{\phi}(\rho^{0}\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{\tau}+\bar{\rho}_{k}u_{\tau}^{0})+\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{n},

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\bar{p}_{k+1}=-\partial_{t}(\rho^{0}\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{n})+\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\partial_{\phi}\left\{\rho^{0}u_{\tau}^{0}(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{n})+T^{0}\langle\mathcal{A}_{12}^{0},(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{0})\bar{f}_{k}\rangle\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\mathcal{H}^{\varepsilon}(y)\left\{(2\rho^{0}u_{\tau}^{0}\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{\tau}+\bar{\rho}_{k}|u_{\tau}^{0}|^{2})+2T^{0}\langle\mathcal{A}_{22}^{0},(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{0})\bar{f}_{k}\rangle\right\}

(2.9)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-T^{0}\partial_{y}\langle\mathcal{A}_{11}^{0},(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{0})\bar{f}_{k+1}\rangle.

The boundary condition of (
2.8
) and (
2.9
) is given

(2.10)

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to\infty}\bar{u}_{k+1}\cdot\vec{n}=0,\quad\lim_{y\to\infty}\bar{p}_{k+1}\cdot\vec{n}=0.

For brevity, the explicit calculations leading to (
2.7
) are omitted here. As the derivation involves geometric terms, the full details are provided in Appendix 6.1 to ensure completeness.

Remark 2.7
.

To solve (
1.22
), the problem equivalently reduces to solving the linear parabolic system (
2.7
)–(
2.10
). As an initial-boundary value problem, proper boundary condition must be specified for (
2.7
). As mentioned in Remark
2.4
, the boundary data cannot be arbitrarily assigned. One can see the details on obtaining boundary condition in Section 2.4.

Remark 2.8
.

With suitable boundary condition, the existence of smooth solution of parabolic equations (
2.7
) can be found in Section 3.2. Subsequently, with prior knowledge of
(\bar{u}_{k}\cdot\vec{n})
and
\bar{p}_{k}
, the residual component
(\mathbf{I-P}_{0})\bar{f}_{k+1}
can be uniquely determined by (
1.25
).

2.3.
Reformulation of Knudsen boundary layers

To solve (
1.32
), we decompose
\hat{S}_{k}=\hat{S}_{k,1}+\hat{S}_{k,2}
with

(2.11)
